A base is only what was recorded: the GitHub native stack of the branch's open pull request, else that pull request's base, else the branch's reflog (its `branch: Created from <ref>` entry, or for `Created from HEAD` the worktree's HEAD reflog `checkout: moving from <prev>` at that time), accepted only with one candidate, the parent neither renamed into nor recreated since (a parent renamed since is followed to its name), and the branch not rebased onto anything outside the parent since; else the chain ends at the remote default branch. The chain follows parents to that branch, a cycle or 10 links. Ancestry only measures each link: its fork point is `merge-base <tip> HEAD`, kept only when it is a range start the `changes` route accepts (otherwise the link has none, is marked incomplete and offers no diff since, so the routes' range boundary is never widened); behind is `rev-list --count HEAD..<tip>`; and each link lists the IDs of its commits on HEAD's line between the next link's fork point and its own, at most 200; every listed commit comes with its first parent, subject, time and the paths it changes (at most 300; a merge's against its first parent), so the tree names commits past the list's 200 and its filter finds a commit by a file. A GitHub member is measured at its head commit when this clone has it, else at a local branch at that commit, else not at all. Members above the current branch get only `lacks` (`rev-list --count <tip>..HEAD`), because their commits are not on HEAD's line and the range routes refuse them. `main` gives the default branch's behind count and the mtime of `git rev-parse --git-path FETCH_HEAD` in the worktree, so "last fetch here". GitHub is read by one read-only GraphQL query (`readPullStack` in `packages/github/src/stack.ts`; mechanics M163) through the `gh` of the GitHub source covering the repository, else the default `gh`, for the work item's linked pull request, else the branch's open pull request in `upstream` or `origin` with `origin`'s owner as head, so a fork's is found; several open matches make the link unknown. That GitHub phase has a 3 s deadline and never holds the answer past 0.8 s: the hub answers from git with `github.state` `pending` and the page asks once more after the deadline. A successful observation is used for 5 minutes, and as `stale` when a later read fails, before the reflog is; a sign-in or rate-limit failure holds off GitHub for a minute. The git answer is cached by HEAD's name and commit, every local and remote ref with its upstream tracking, and the times of the reflogs and `FETCH_HEAD`; Refresh passes both caches; reads of one state are shared, and a read whose state changed meanwhile runs again. Work items are looked up after the cache, at each answer.
  - `GET /v1/conversation/tree?window=<page key>` (`PageTree` in `packages/hub/src/tree.ts`) answers `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`, sorted, at most 20,000 paths with `more`.
  - `GET /v1/conversation/search?window=<page key>&gen=<n>&q=<text>[&case=1][&word=1][&regex=1][&glob=<pattern>]` runs `git grep --untracked --exclude-standard -I`, literal unless `regex`, ignoring case unless `case`, so ignored files never match. `glob` (`*`, `**`, `?`) is matched by the hub on each result's path, since the hub's git takes literal pathspecs. The search stops git at 2,000 lines, 200 files, 1 MiB or 10 s and says `more`. One search runs per page key: a newer `gen` stops the one running, an older one is refused 409 without stopping anything, and a regular expression git refuses answers 400 with git's message.
- **Diffs** (PRD R118). Every changed file's diff shows in one Changes tab, the strip's only Changes item, which has no close mark; its list, its collapsed files and Hide viewed last until the page leaves the conversation (and its scope), the diffs are drawn again when the tab is chosen again, and nothing of it is kept in the hub. A bar above the diffs holds the list's note, its toggles and a Files menu of the rows to go to one, so no second strip item duplicates the tab. `GET /v1/conversation/diff?window=<page key>&path=<path>` (`readDiff`) answers git's patch and both whole sides for a path that is one of the change rows now (400 for a path that is not plain and relative, 404 for one that is no row): the old side read only at the row's own path in the range's start commit, the new side at its path in the range's end commit, never a ref the page names, or for a range to disk by real path inside the worktree, refusing any link or junction on the way, even one that stays inside it, since it could lead to an ignored file or to git's own. Sides and patches are read only up to a cap, so a file a session grows while it is read is still cut there, and a binary file is sent without bytes; `sides=0` answers the patch alone. Its `ETag` lets a refetched diff that did not change answer 304. Reading every change costs seconds in a large worktree, so the `diff` and `file` routes answer from the page's last read of its changes (`PageChanges`, kept by page key with its scope and range, a read on its way shared) rather than read them all again for each file (mechanics M158), while it still holds: HEAD, the base refs and the index file are as it read them, so the commits and the staged changes with their renames are too, and, for a range to disk, each of the row's paths changes in the working tree as it did then, read by `git status` for those paths alone, so a file ignored, deleted, put back or made again since fails it while an edit that leaves it modified does not. A path the read does not have, a binary row, a row whose patch is now empty, or a read that no longer holds is answered by a fresh read, kept unless a newer list read was kept meanwhile, so 404 still means no row now and a commit since the list, or a chosen commit no longer on the branch, is found as before. The patch and the sides are always read at the request, and the `file` route's own checks of a disk file stay as they are. A binary file, a generated one (a lock, minified, snapshot or build-output file) and one past 1,500 changed lines or too large for the hub to count start collapsed and are read only when expanded, so one such file never holds up the rest. While the focus is in the tab, single-letter keys move between files and changes and tick or collapse the file at the top, and Escape leaves a file view for the diffs where they were. In a pane under 600 px, as on a phone or in the side column, a file's Viewed tick shows as its box alone, at least 44 px square on a touch screen, so the file's name keeps the header's room. The tab is one scrolling view of every row, in the list's order (`packages/web/src/diff/viewer.ts`); the page reads only the patches of the files near the view (`changesWindow.ts`) and holds a bounded amount of diff text, so a large change opens as fast as a small one, and it never diffs the sides itself. The layout and wrapping follow the settings `web.diffLayout` and `web.diffWrap` ([Settings](#settings)), which every page reads from its stream. The bar's Wrap toggle, the one diff control the bar keeps while a file view shows, shows whether long lines wrap now and flips it at once for the diffs and the file view; its first use keeps the choice in that device's local storage, where it overrides `web.diffWrap`, because a paired device cannot change settings ([Remote access](#remote-access)), and a device with no choice kept follows the setting, Auto included. `diff` takes `ws=ignore` (`git diff -w`); a file that then has no patch answers `same: true`, which the page says in its place, and the mode is part of the key a held diff is kept by. Each click on a hunk separator shows 20 more unchanged lines. Lines selected in a diff or the file view can be quoted into the composer's draft as a fenced block named by path, lines, side and revision; nothing is stored. The diff viewer (@pierre/diffs with Shiki highlighting in module workers; [mechanics.md](mechanics.md)) holds a view shorter than its pane at the pane's top, which CodeView 1.6.4's sticky positioning would push down by a varying amount (`keepAtTop` in `packages/web/src/diff/viewer.ts` wraps a private CodeView method, and its test fails if an upgrade removes it); it is its own ES modules, loaded the first time a diff shows, so the page's first load does not grow. Activation and `hub.js install` install it as `<home>/web/diff/<build>/` (`installDiffViewer` in `packages/hub/src/pageAssets.ts`), and the hub serves `/page/diff/<build>/<file>` (cookie) only for an installed build and a plain relative `.js` or `.json` file its manifest lists, cached as immutable.
- **File view and Open in VS Code** (PRD R118). A line number clicked in a diff shows that file read-only at the line, the new side on disk, or in the range's end commit, and the old side at the range's start. `GET /v1/conversation/file?window=<page key>&path=<path>&side=disk|base` (`readFileView`) answers it with an `ETag`: on disk, only a file `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard` lists, so never an ignored file such as an `.env` or a file under `.git`, read by real path inside the worktree; at the base, only a change row's own path at the range's start, and for a range to a commit, `disk` only a change row's own path in that commit; capped, a binary file without bytes; 400 for a path that is not plain and relative or another side, 404 for any other file. The answer gives the file's size and, for a picture or PDF whose extension and leading bytes agree, its media type; `GET /v1/conversation/file-raw` sends such a file's bytes under the same checks, with the artifacts' sandbox CSP, so files reached from the tree use only these routes and never the local file routes, which keep their own roots for links in the conversation. A disk read to disk reads no change list: it checks the folder is its repository's top, lists the path with `ls-files` and reads it, so reading it again costs one listing and one read. The page's file view (`packages/web/src/fileView.tsx`, shared with local file tabs, which keep their own reading, refusals and navigation) shows code through the diff viewer's file mode, Markdown as a preview with Source (opened at a line, the source), and pictures and PDFs; a Markdown link or picture resolves only to a path of the worktree: a picture is read through `file-raw` at the view's revision, a link opens its file on disk through `file`, and any other target, an absolute path or a `file:` URI, is drawn as inert text with why (`markdown.ts`'s denied answer), never as a local file link. A view on disk is read again as a local file tab is, on the page's one file timer, a turn's end, a reconnect and the page showing, with the same rule that a periodic read leaves a slow read alone and an asked-for read replaces it; a view at a commit or the base cannot change and is read once. A view of a side of a row the list shows offers that row's diff. Open in VS Code, which opens or brings forward the work item's window on its worktree, is the work item's Actions menu control (`row-command`, Open in VS Code, above); the page opens no single file in VS Code.
- **Local files.** A link in the conversation to a file or folder on this computer (a `file:` URI, an absolute path, or a path relative to the current session's working folder) is read through `GET /v1/conversation/local-file?window=<page key>&path=<target>` (`packages/hub/src/localFiles.ts`), and a picture's or PDF's bytes through `local-file-raw`. A target is read only when its real path lies inside the conversation's roots: its own folder, the working folder of each session of its work item, which holds its `.wip` and artifact folders, its ticket's worktree with that worktree's main checkout and the repository root the ticket resolves to, and the Claude scratchpad of each of those sessions; and, as a single regular file, the selected work item's approval draft while it awaits approval, which Open report opens, on a paired device too, and nothing whose real path lies in the Switchboard home, which holds the hub's keys and sign-ins, through any root; a link or junction out of a root, another session's scratchpad, any path through a `.git` folder and a root that is a drive or filesystem root are refused. The read goes through the file handle opened after the check and only while the checked path still names that file, so a file swapped for a link meanwhile is refused. Text is capped and sent with a binary file named but not sent; folders list a bounded number of entries; the raw route sends only pictures and PDFs, an SVG under the artifacts' sandbox CSP, so no local file runs as a document of the page's origin. The key must be held by an open page stream of the same sign-in and from the same place, this computer or the remote host and login. A remote device reads less, because these routes read beyond the files git lists (the operator's decision): only a file, never a folder's listing, whose real path lies inside the working folder the selected conversation's current session runs in, or the checkout before its first session, or the work item's checkouts: its ticket's worktree, that worktree's main checkout and the repository root the ticket resolves to, and the approval draft as above; another session's folder, the conversation's host folder, scratchpads, and a link, junction or `..` out of that folder are refused with `LOCAL_FILES_PAIRED_ONLY`, which the page shows in the file tab. The routes only read, on either device. A file tab in view, its strip's tab shown on a visible page and not under a phone's list, follows its file: the page reads it again periodically (`FILE_POLL_MS` in `packages/web/src/main.ts`), when the tab is chosen or its link clicked again, after a turn ends, once a reconnect's new stream shows the work item, and when the page shows again, sending the `ETag` the route gave, a hash of the view with its own value for the gzipped bytes, so an unchanged file answers 304 and nothing is drawn; an answer read for an earlier page key or scope generation, as before a reconnect or a change of session, is dropped; a picture's or PDF's view carries its modification time, which its URL carries too, so a new version loads. A tab is read once at a time: a read asked for while another is on its way, by a choice, a turn's end, a reconnect or the page showing again, is made when that read answers, and that answer is dropped, so the tab shows the file as it is after the ask; a periodic read leaves the read on its way to answer. A new version keeps the reader's place, code by replacing its text in the file view it is in. A read again that fails for any reason other than the file being gone keeps what the tab shows, as while the hub restarts; a file gone shows why, and comes back when it does. Polling, not a hub watch, because the hub does not know which file tabs a page has open, and a page reads only the one tab in view. A Markdown link to a path or a `file:` URI is drawn as such a link (an http or https link opens as a web page, and any other scheme stays text), so a `file:` URI (`file:///D:/a.ts`, `file:///home/a.ts`, `file://localhost/…` or a UNC `file://host/share/…`), which markdown-it would otherwise refuse and leave as text, is a link too; the page sends the hub the path it names, percent-decoded, and on a paired device a target outside what it may read is still a link whose tab shows the refusal. Once a read passes those checks, the answer names the work item's artifact the file is, by real path (`artifact`: a file a tag named, or an artifact the artifact folder lists whose real path is the file's, so an artifact that links to a deeper file of the folder names that file; `pageArtifactAt` in `packages/hub/src/conversation.ts`), and the page shows that artifact's tab in place of the file tab; a refused read names none, so it tells a page nothing it may not read. A click or Enter on such a link opens the file in a closable file tab of its own, for as long as the page shows the conversation: Markdown drawn as an artifact is, with its relative links and pictures resolved against its own folder, a picture or PDF as an artifact is, code and text in the diff viewer's file view at the line the link names (`#L<n>` or `:<n>`), following the device's Wrap as the Changes tab does, and a folder as a list whose entries open tabs.
- **Viewed ticks** (PRD R118). A tick given from the tree's uncommitted files is posted for `from=HEAD&to=worktree`, which shares the ticks of every range to disk, so a list to disk shows it too. Each change row carries `hash`, its content on disk as git's blob ID, or `unreadable` or `deleted`, and `viewed`, whether the work item's `viewedFiles` rows in the store (dropped with the work item) hold that same hash, so a tick clears by itself once the file changes, as on a pull request. A range to a commit gives each row its blob ID in that commit, and its ticks are kept under the range's two commit IDs, apart from the ticks of every range to disk, which share theirs since their hash is the file on disk. `viewed {window, scopeGen, opId, path, hash, viewed[, from, to]}` carries the hash the page shows and the range it lists: the hub reads the changes again and keeps the tick only while the row's hash is that one, else 409, which the page answers by taking its tick back and reading the list again. Every other page showing the work item gets `viewed {workItemId, path, hash, viewed, scope}`, which a page applies only while it lists that scope,, so a tick on the phone shows on the desktop page. Ticking a file collapses it to its header and, when it was the file at the top, brings the next shown file up, so reading goes on downward; unticking or a refused tick expands it in place. Viewed files start collapsed, a file the operator expanded stays so while the tab is open, another device's tick collapses a file only while it is off screen, and a file collapsed for its tick expands once the tick goes.
- **File type icons.** The Changes rows, the artifact and file tabs and their rows in the More menu draw each file's icon from VS Code's Seti file icon theme, the default of VS Code's file icon themes, in its dark or light colours to match the scheme. An artifact's card in the conversation, its row in the Artifacts tab and the head of an open artifact show a codicon for how the page shows the file instead, named for screen readers: Markdown document, HTML page, picture, recording, video, PDF, text or data, or another file (`artifactType` in `packages/web/src/artifactIcon.tsx`), each with its title and how long ago it changed; a head opened in a new window shows the full time instead, since that window is not redrawn. The page carries a copy of the theme with the MIT notices of Seti UI and VS Code, and picks an icon as VS Code ranks its icon theme's rules (mechanics M110). The page loads the mapping and font the first time it draws a file's icon, and a file shows the `file` codicon until then, and for good when they do not load.
- **Attached files.** `POST /v1/conversation/upload?window=<page key>&scopeGen=<n>&name=<file name>` (cookie) takes one file's bytes as its body, from the page's own `Origin`, with or without `Content-Length`, for a page key whose selected conversation's current session can take a message, at the `scopeGen` the page showed (403 or 409 otherwise). The hub accepts at most 10 MB (413): it refuses a declared larger length before reading, and stops keeping bytes beyond the limit when no length is given. It is not a conversation command, so it is not in `PAGE_COMMANDS`, and the hub token reaches none. `writeUpload` (`packages/hub/src/uploads.ts`) writes the file to `<os temp>/switchboard-pasted/`, outside Windows `switchboard-pasted-<user ID>/` readable only by the user, refusing a folder another user owns: a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP picture, known by its first bytes rather than its name or type, named `pasted-<time>-<random>`, and any other file under a generated name that keeps a sanitized form of its own. It refuses an empty file, and one named as a picture that is not one, since Claude reads a file with that extension as a picture (400); it never writes over a file, refuses a folder that is a link or junction, and deletes only its own files there older than a week. It answers `{path, image}`. The composer attaches files picked, pasted or dropped, at most 10 to a message; it retries an upload once when the browser could not complete the request, but not when the hub refused it. The retry is marked in the hub log; if the first request wrote the file but its response was lost, the unused copy stays until the normal cleanup. The message carries each file as a mention after the typed text, its path written as Claude Code's panel writes a mention, so Claude reads a picture as one (mechanics M88). Attachments belong to their work item and are kept in local storage over a reload until a message that names them is taken.
- **Focus.** The page posts `page-focus {window, focused}` when it gains or loses the focus or is shown or hidden, `focused` being whether a visible page has the focus; a focused page is the focus the voice follows ([Voice](#voice)), so its selected work item's current session is a voice target. A page's own blur does not clear another page's focus. Closing the page ends its stream and its focus. The extension host keeps its trusted app child informed of foreground changes.
- **The page** (`packages/web/src`), phone first: in the phone's layout the list and the conversation are each a full-width view, with Back from the conversation to the list. The page draws it under 768 px wide, or on a device whose screen's short side is under 500 px, so a phone keeps it on its side, where it is 667 to 956 px wide; tablets' short sides are 744 px and more, and a desktop gets it by its width alone (`isPhone` in `packages/web/src/layout.ts`, the operator's decision). The page's script decides it and gives the stylesheets a class on the document, so every rule for the phone's layout follows the one definition. In the phone's layout the conversation's header is one 44 px row; on a phone on its side (landscape and at most 500 px tall) the header and the tab strip share that row, half each, and the composer is one row, its Attach, slash commands and context meter left of the box and its voice controls and Send right of it, so the timeline keeps most of the height (about 290 of an iPhone's 393 px, against 175 with the header, the strip and a two-row composer stacked). The composer's box grows with its text up to 200 px or a third of the height that shows, the keyboard's room left out where the browser reports it, whichever is less. A swipe right over the conversation does what Back does, through the same `back()` in `main.ts` (so leaving turns the mic off once, as Back does), because the page needs a back gesture away from the left edge (`swipeBack.ts`): the conversation follows the finger by transforms over the list, and a release past a third of the width or a flick right goes back, else it slides back. A pan counts only when it is clearly horizontal and rightward and does not start in a control, a menu, a sheet or a pane that scrolls sideways, such as code, a table or a diff; on iOS, in the Home Screen app or a browser tab, the browser handles the left-edge swipe when the browser has more than one history entry, so the page leaves that touch alone and does not draw a second list behind the native preview (M133); without such an entry, or on other browsers, the page handles the edge swipe itself. Reduced motion keeps the list still and lands the release at once. A conversation opened from the list on a phone also adds a history entry over the list's, whose address is `#list`, so the browser's back, as Android's back button, goes through `back()` too, and its forward applies the conversation entry's route (Route and restore); Back takes that entry with it, so the browser's back from the list leaves the page as before. A phone that starts on a conversation with no entry of its own, as from a link or an installed app's start, puts the list's entry under it, and a reload of a conversation's entry keeps both entries and adds none, so the browser's back still shows the list. Outside the phone's layout opening a work item adds no history entry (the operator's decision), so the browser's back leaves the page. Outside the phone's layout the list is pinned or unpinned by the pin at its header's end, as VS Code's panes are: pinned, it is a side column at every width; unpinned, it is a drawer while a work item is open, and the side column while none is. The pin shows only while it would change the list, with a work item's conversation open, so it is never a button that does nothing. The pin is the only way between the two: a drag on the list's sash only sets its width, between its least and most, stopping where the conversation keeps its least width and, while the History and Artifacts column shows, where the conversation and that column keep theirs, so neither a drag nor the page's width turns the list into a drawer (the operator's decision, replacing a snap that hid the list when its sash was dragged past its least width). History and Artifacts are a column beside the conversation only while it and the conversation fit beside a pinned list, and tabs otherwise (`fitLayout` in `packages/web/src/layout.ts`); the conversation counts at the width set on its sash, which goes down to 400 px, but at most 640 px and 640 px with none set (`chatNeed`), so narrowing it gives its room to the column. A column comes back only a little past where it fits, so a width at the edge does not flap. The column's strip ends in a maximize button, which lays the column over the whole page, list and conversation too, for a wide artifact or diff, until the same button or Escape restores it or the column folds into tabs; the list and the conversation keep their places under it, and the choice is kept nowhere. Unpinned and hidden, the list leaves a 1 px sliver at the page's edge showing each row's stripe at its height, cut to the list's view and left out under a pinned heading; the pointer resting on it for 0.5 s shows the drawer, sliding in over 0.15 s, and it hides 0.4 s after nothing keeps it open (the pointer over it, an open menu or dialog, typing in its search box) and on choosing a work item. The drawer is also shown by the header's Work items button and, on a touch screen, a swipe right from the page's left edge; shown by the button, a key or a swipe, a backdrop dims the page under it and takes the press outside that closes it, so the press does nothing else. The drawer and its backdrop lie over every bar, sash and menu of the page, and the sashes over the top bars, so each sash can be dragged along the page's whole height; the page's layers follow one z-index scale in `page.css`. The pin, and the widths set on the list's and the conversation's sashes, are kept per browser in local storage, so each device keeps its own; a drag the browser cancels keeps nothing. The list is drawn as List, Groups and Rows above say, and the conversation, its header and the composer as the [Conversation view](#conversation-view) says. The composer sends to the session it shows with its `scopeGen` and `launch`, keeps a draft per work item in local storage, and takes back the text of a message that was not sent, into that work item's draft even when the page shows another; a card's refused answer unlocks its card. A Codex session's pending questions and approvals are answered with `answer`; one the page cannot answer says to answer it in the terminal. Answers to a select, history page or picker that arrive after the page moved on are dropped, and the latest work item clicked is the one selected. In the wide layout the conversation's pane always shows its composer; where the tabs fold into the conversation's, as on a phone, the composer and the ended session's note above it show only on the Chat tab, at any height, while the mic is off (`#root.off-conversation` in `page.css`, set by `main.ts`). They are hidden by the stylesheet rather than removed, so a hidden composer keeps its draft, attachments and live words, and the voice process plays a reply on; a focused box gives up the focus as it hides, so the hub learns of the blur and dictation sends spoken turns again. A send that ends off the Chat tab there says Sent in the toast, and files dropped on another tab show the Chat tab, with their chips. While the mic is on for the session shown (the page's voice view here, in any mic state but off and idle-off, or a Mic on tap still starting) and the session can take a message, the page sets `#root.dictating` and leaves `off-conversation` off, so the same composer, with its draft, mic button and live words, shows below every tab, which ends above it rather than lying under it, so no tab's content, Console's keys bar or the Changes controls hide; a maximized column ends above the conversation's composer by its measured height, so it shows there too (the operator's decision, so that dictation never runs unseen). It hides again at once after a Mic off from its own button, and 1.5 s (`DICTATION_GRACE_MS`) after the mic goes off by any other means, unless the mic comes back on meanwhile, so a passing state does not move the tab; another work item drops it at once. An ended session's note shows its reason and **Resume** above the composer. `resume {window, scopeGen, opId, sessionId}` takes only a page key and its selected conversation's current session once it has ended (403 otherwise, 409 for a stale `scopeGen` or a session that has not ended) and resumes it without a terminal: a Code Mode session through the attach terminal's own path with its reservation and refusals, the reservation lapsing as for an attach no terminal took; a Codex app-server session through `resumeThread`, as a message does; any other with 409. The composer's footer has the voice controls, drawn by one renderer (`packages/conversation/src/voiceControls.ts`), from the page's own voice state only. They control this computer's one voice process: the mic posts `mic`, and the box posts `composer {focused, draft, listen: true}` for the focus rule, so focusing it keeps the mic listening into its draft ([Dictation](#dictation)). On this computer's page the microphone and speaker are the computer's, not the browser's; on a paired device's page the mic is the device's own, used while it has the voice ([Remote access](#remote-access), Dictation on the phone). When dictation goes to the page, or to another page on the same session, live words show after its box's text, and a `draft` for its session fills the box only while the box is empty or still holds the hub's previous draft for that session, never over text typed after it; each piece the hub joined goes to the end of the box's text instead. Unread replies and the state are marked seen once visible on a focused page ([Unread](#unread)).
- **Tabs.** The column's strip and, on a narrower page, the conversation's are drawn as VS Code's editor tabs, each with its icon before its name; an embedded link tab ([Embedded link tabs](#embedded-link-tabs)) shows an icon for what it opens and a label naming the item (PRD R125; `frameLink` in `packages/web/src/iframeBrowser.ts`): `#<n>` after GitHub's mark or the pull request icon for a GitHub issue or pull request, read from its URL whatever named it, the ticket's key without its source's prefix after the source's mark, where the list has one, for another source's ticket, and the link's own name, else its host, after the globe for any other page. Every tab, fixed, link, artifact or file tab alike, always shows its icon and its label, never an icon alone. The tabs share the strip, each at most as wide as its icon, label and marks, a label at most about 100 px; a fixed tab (Chat, Changes, History, Browser, Console) always shows its whole label and never shrinks, because a cut label of a fixed tab saved too little room to be worth the guess (operator decision 2026-11-26); a strip without room for them all takes room from the widest link, artifact or file tab first until all are as wide, then shrinks those alike, cutting their labels with an ellipsis, but never below a tab's least width, its label cut to about five characters and the ellipsis (`--sb-tab-label-least` in page.css). On a touch screen a close mark is a 44 px target, counted in its tab's least width. Both strips draw their tabs from one ordered list (`stripTabs` in `packages/web/src/tabs.ts`), so they show the same tabs in the same order at every width: Chat (in the conversation's strip only), Changes ([Changes](#web-page)), first because it is the work item's main view, History, one tab per supported link while embedded tabs are enabled, the artifacts newest first, the file tabs in the order they were opened, then Console (while the conversation has an active session the terminal holder may hold). The tabs form one group; at the strip's end follow **More** and, in the column, the maximize button. **Fitting.** The strip shows the tabs whose least widths fit it (`fitTabs`, measured after each draw and again when the strip's width changes): all of them when they fit, else, beside More, the selected tab, Chat, Changes, History, then the rest in strip order while they fit; a strip too narrow for those drops History first, then Changes, then Chat, and always keeps the selected tab, so every tab stays reachable through More. On a phone about 390 px wide this gives Chat, History, the selected tab and More. More shows while some tabs do not fit or some artifacts are closed: its count, with the unread dot on the count's corner while any row of its menu is unread, and no caret, since it is named as a menu (`aria-haspopup`) and by its tooltip. Its menu lists every tab the strip does not show, in strip order and Changes and Console among them, each with its icon, its label and an artifact's age in columns, then the closed artifacts newest first; a row does what its tab does, and Up and Down move through it as in the other menus. The keyboard's Left and Right follow the strip's order. A link tab's tooltip is its full name, as the list's pill names it, and its URL; an artifact tab's is its title, its type and its age. An artifact is unread while this device has not shown its current version, so a changed artifact is unread again (PRD R99): its tab carries the unread dot ([Unread](#unread)) at its label's top-right, as History's tab does, taking no width from the tab, and says unread in its name, and its More row carries the dot after its title and says unread in its row's name; a hidden History whose state is unread is marked the same way. Each device keeps, by work item, each artifact's path and the version (`updatedAt`) it last showed, in local storage, each page merging its record with its other pages' by the newer version, and the artifacts a work item has when the device first shows it count as shown, so the mark needs no clock of the device's; an artifact counts as shown once its tab shows, not under the phone's list, and a new version that comes while it shows is shown too. A path an earlier page kept without its version counts as shown at the version the work item's next `hello` has. The hub records no artifact as seen, so an artifact opened on one device stays new on another. Closing an artifact's tab hides it for the work item on every device until a newer version of the artifact opens it again (a choice: a new version is worth seeing); More lists the closed artifacts after the tabs it holds, and choosing one opens its tab again. **Opening and closing.** Each document has one tab, by its id (`artifact:<path>`, `file:<real path>`, `link:<url>`), and every way in opens it through one function, `openDoc` in main.ts, which selects the tab it has: a conversation or History link, Open report, an artifact card, a More row and the route. A file the hub names as an artifact gives way to that artifact's tab once read, so it shows its file tab's loading state until then, and every later link to it reads it again, so the hub checks each open. Only a file tab the page shows takes the page to the artifact; one the operator has left gives way unseen, and one kept as the column's choice on a narrower page leaves the artifact as that choice; a file read again by another spelling gives way to the tab it has. Closing the tab shown (`closeDoc`), a link, artifact or file, shows its neighbour among those in strip order, the one before it, else the one after it, and with none left History, or the conversation on a narrower page; closing another tab changes nothing else. What shows next is no choice of the operator's, so it posts no `page-tabs` and leaves the kept tab of every device as it was. A tab that goes, as Console when the session ends, an artifact that goes away or the artifact shown when another device closes it, shows History, or the conversation on a narrower page, in its place, and keeps no choice either. A restored file tab the hub names as an artifact gives way to that artifact's tab as no choice too, and leaves an artifact closed on every device closed. **Kept choices.** The hub keeps in the store each work item's tab of Console or an artifact and its closed artifact tabs (`pageTabs`), and one choice for every work item, History or Browser to open on (`pagePrefs`, `openOn`; `status`, kept or sent before History took the Status tab's place, is read and kept as `history`), all shared by every device. `page-tabs {window, workItemId, tab?, close?, reopen?, openOn?}` (cookie and page key, 403 for another work item than the page's, 400 for another tab or body) keeps the tab, closes one artifact's tab at its version or opens one again, one artifact at a time so devices closing different tabs do not overwrite each other, keeps a bounded number of closed tabs, keeps `openOn`, and sends the work item's `tabs {workItemId, tabs}` to every page showing it, the sender's with its request's `opId`, so the stream's last `tabs` is the hub's latest whichever device's request came first; a page's `hello` carries its work item's `tabs` and `openOn`. A `tabs` event only reconciles the closed artifacts and moves no tab but the one closed: a page reads it with its own latest close or reopen of each artifact over it until the event with that request's `opId` comes, so neither an echo of the page's own earlier change nor another device's older event undoes a later change, even one that holds the same state. Opening a work item shows the tabs this device left it on (Route and restore), else its kept Console or artifact while that still applies, else History or Browser as PRD R125 chooses, leaving `openOn` as it was; that is the column's tab, and where the column folds into the conversation's strip, the page shows the Chat tab on every opening (PRD R109), from the list, the header's list, a link, Back and forward or a reload, also of the work item already open.
- **Phones first.** The conversation pane works alone at a phone's width (375 px), and on a touch screen every control is at least 44 px with nothing shown only on hover. A conversation pane under 600 px wide, on a phone or in a narrow column, gives its text the width: less padding at its sides, a narrower gutter for the replies' dots and bubbles that run nearly to the far side, while a wider pane keeps the margins messaging apps leave.
- **Drawing.** The page is drawn with Preact components, so text and attributes are escaped by default and each region updates on its own, changing only the nodes that differ, so the focus, a text selection, the caret, scroll positions and open menus stay. Menus are placed as the Conversation view's ([Conversation view](#conversation-view), Menus). A row's menu in the list is fixed to the viewport at its row and rises above the conversation and its column while open, so the list's scroll box never clips it where it passes the list's edge. Markup the page does not build itself, markdown-it's Markdown with raw HTML off and the HTML of the shared renderers in `packages/conversation`, becomes nodes through `trusted` (`src/trusted.tsx`), the one part allowed to parse HTML, which drops event handler attributes; it is never given the hub's or the operator's text. A `.tsx` part uses no API that parses HTML or sets markup directly, so JSX escapes everything it draws (`escaping.test.ts`). Stream events draw at most once per frame, and a draw renders only what changed, so its script time barely grows with the conversation's length (mechanics M167): the conversation skips its redraw when none of its inputs changed (`timelineInputs` in `src/timeline.tsx`), each message, reply and list row draws again only when its own props change, Markdown and parsed HTML are kept by their text, the least recently used dropped past a long conversation's worth or 16 million characters, and a text over 64 K characters, such as an artifact's document, never kept, and a reply's progress while it plays and dictation's live words ask for no draw. An open artifact's drawn document is kept only for the current scope, up to a few.
- **Layers.** Every sheet and dialog (a row's sheets, the list header's sheets, Settings and its questions) is modal (`modalLayers` in `packages/web/src/layers.ts`). While one is open, the rest of the page is inert but the toast, the connection screen and the live region, so Tab and presses reach only the top one and those; the toast and the Update ready card lie over every sheet and dialog, so their buttons still take a press. The top one takes the focus on its first control unless it places the focus itself; every Escape goes to it, also one pressed with the focus fallen to the page's body or on the toast; and closing it gives the focus back to the control that opened it, for one opened from a menu that menu's button or row, while that control is still there and enabled. The list header's sheets are drawn at the page's root like the others, so neither the drawer's stacking nor a phone's hidden list view covers or hides them.
- **Markdown** (`src/markdown.ts`). Replies, states, Markdown artifacts and local Markdown files are drawn as GitHub-flavoured Markdown: tables with their alignment, task lists with read-only checkboxes, strikethrough, autolinked web and mail addresses, footnotes, emoji shortcodes, GitHub's alerts (`> [!NOTE]`, `[!TIP]`, `[!IMPORTANT]`, `[!WARNING]`, `[!CAUTION]`) with their icon, title and colour in either scheme, a `diff` block's lines coloured, Mermaid diagrams, and collapsible `<details>` sections with a `<summary>` and Markdown inside, nested too. Raw HTML stays off: the renderer's own rules read only `<details>`, `<details open>`, `<summary>`, `<br>`, comments (hidden) and a short list of inline tags such as `<kbd>`, `<sub>` and `<sup>`, each without attributes, so a tag with attributes, an unpaired one or any other stays text, but for `<img>` with only `src`, `alt`, `title`, `width` and `height`, rebuilt and its source resolved as a Markdown picture's, and a `<p>` or `<div>` with at most `align`, which shows nothing so a README's centred pictures show, and a `<details>` left open is closed where its container ends, so no tag reaches outside its place. A heading's anchor and a footnote's place are `data-anchor` marks, not ids, and a `#name` link scrolls to its mark in the same document without an `href`, so the page's ids and the route in its address are never touched; an artifact opened in its own window gets them back as ids and fragment links. Every link, to a web page, a file or a place in the text, is the theme's link colour, visited or not, and always underlined, with the focus ring on keyboard focus. Bullets are a disc at the root, a circle and then a square below it, as in Claude's VS Code extension, also where the Markdown sits in a list such as the timeline. A state's body, artifacts and local files are one kind of Markdown document (`md-doc`), with one typography; a state adds only its head line and headline. A picture the page cannot show, because its source names nothing the page may read, the hub refuses it, or it is on the web, which the page's CSP blocks, leaves a note in its place naming it by its alt text or file name, with a link to open a web picture in a browser tab. Syntax colouring of code blocks, math, and GitHub's mentions and issue and commit references are not drawn.
### Remote access

PRD R115: the [web page](#web-page) is reachable from the operator's phone or another of their devices through `tailscale serve`, which takes HTTPS on this machine's Tailscale name, for the tailnet only, and proxies it to the page's fixed port (`web.port`); `tailscale funnel` is never used. The hub still listens on `127.0.0.1` only and serves nothing to the network itself.

- **Hosts and logins.** Remote access is on only with at least one allowed host (`web.remoteHosts`) and one allowed Tailscale login (`web.remoteLogins`); with `web.tailscale` on, the hub adds this machine's Tailscale name and its owner's login from `tailscale status --json` while Tailscale is running (`packages/hub/src/tailscale.ts`). The hub reads these at start and again whenever one of the keys changes, without a restart. A change ends the open streams of every remote device whose host or login is no longer allowed and refuses its later requests, but keeps its sign-in, so allowing it again lets it back in without pairing.
- **Which requests.** The hub classifies every page request before any route (`pageOrigin` in `packages/hub/src/page.ts`). A peer other than the loopback is refused. A request without any header that only a proxy adds is this computer's own and must name `127.0.0.1:<port>` or `localhost:<port>`, against DNS rebinding. A request with any proxy header is never this computer's own: it is a remote request only when its `Host` is an allowed host, it arrived as HTTPS, it is not a Funnel request, and its `Tailscale-User-Login` is an allowed login; anything else is refused with 403 and the reason. This relies on `tailscale serve` setting the `Tailscale-User-*` headers from the connecting device's owner, removing any a client sent, and sending none for a tagged device, a Funnel request or this machine. A remote request's `Origin` must be its own HTTPS origin, and its responses carry HSTS.
- **Pairing.** A remote device signs in only by pairing, which only this computer starts. A pairing gives a one-time link and a short code for the first remote host, both short-lived; using either uses up both, and too many wrong codes drop every outstanding pairing. A pairing token works only through its remote host and a local token only on this computer; a token tried in the wrong place is used up. A remote host shows a device that is not signed in a page where it types the code. A successful pairing stores a sign-in bound to the device's host and login: it is refused through any other host or login and on this computer, as this computer's own sign-ins are refused through a remote host. Pairing tokens, codes and failed-code counts live in the hub's memory and end with it.
- **Devices.** This computer's page, the hub token's routes, and `hub.js web devices` and `hub.js web revoke [<id>]` list and revoke sign-ins; a sign-in's ID tells nothing of its cookie. A sign-in is named by its device's system and browser, such as "Windows · Edge", read from the browser's User-Agent when it signs in; one whose name lacks either, as a sign-in made before names were kept, takes the name of its next allowed request whose User-Agent names both, and a full name never changes. Revoking a sign-in ends its open streams. The commands in `LOCAL_PAGE_COMMANDS` (`packages/hub/src/server.ts`) are refused with 403 from a remote device, so a paired device cannot pair or sign out devices, allow voice, open a link with this computer's default browser (`row-open`), start VS Code (`row-command`), open a held session's terminal app, restart the terminal holder, or read, change or enrol anything in Settings (the operator's decision: Settings stay on the computer), and its page leaves those controls out, but for a held session's Open in terminal, which shows the refusal; it follows Fold reply details and Show message times and notes as Settings set them ([Conversation view](#conversation-view), Speech). VS Code would start on this computer, not on the device, and a phone or tablet cannot run it, so a paired device's page drops Open in VS Code from every row's menu and the Actions menu, shows History's folder links as text, and its Changes notes for a file too large to show do not point to VS Code; the page answers only on this computer and through `tailscale serve`, so every phone or tablet is a paired device. The [local file routes](#web-page) give a remote device only files in its session's working folder and its work item's worktree and repository root, and the work item's approval draft while it awaits approval. Everything else the page does works from a paired device, and the browser extension's routes and download work with its sign-in ([Browser extension](#browser-extension)).
- **Changes.** A paired device reads the change routes (`changes`, `diff`, `file`, `file-raw`, `stack`, `tree`, `search`) as this computer does: they read only the files git lists in the selected conversation's worktree, tracked or untracked and never ignored, under the checks above, so they need none of the local file routes' narrower paired-device rule. GitHub is read by the hub with its own sign-in; the device sends none.
- **Folders.** A paired device's New session form reaches only folders inside the recent folders, repo roots and worktrees, which the hub checks on every request through it, browsing and creation alike ([Standalone sessions](#standalone-sessions)); this computer's page reaches any folder.
- **Voice on a phone** (PRD R119; [mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M111). Voice is off on every paired device until this computer allows it for that sign-in, so signing the device out or pairing it again forgets the permission; this computer's own sign-in has no voice to allow. The phone's page carries voice over the **voice socket**, a WebSocket on the page's origin at `/v1/voice/socket` (`packages/hub/src/voiceSocket.ts`). The upgrade is checked as a page command is and further: a valid sign-in for that host and login, a matching `Origin`, a page key held by the same sign-in's open page stream, a remote device, `voice.enabled`, and voice allowed on the device. The sign-in is a one-use ticket, not the cookie: the page posts `page-voice-ticket` for its page key, a request that carries the cookie, and names the ticket in the upgrade, because a browser leaves the `SameSite=Strict` cookie off a WebSocket opened in a frame of the browser extension's tab ([mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M154). A ticket is given only for a page key of the asking sign-in, is bound to that key, origin and sign-in, is one of at most four held for the key, the oldest dropped first so that overlapping taps each keep theirs, goes when the key's page stream ends, lapses after 30 s and is used up by the upgrade that names it, refused or not; an upgrade without one is checked by its cookie. Each refusal is an HTTP answer with its reason, never a socket, and is logged; `page-voice-state` tells a page why it may not, because a refused upgrade gives a browser no reason. The socket carries JSON messages and binary audio frames; the hub closes it for protocol violations, rate or queue overruns and silence, and every message re-checks the device's sign-in, host, login and voice permission. One device's page holds the voice at a time: a newer socket takes it and closes the older. The hub closes the socket with the reason when the device loses permission, its sign-in is revoked or replaced, remote access no longer allows it, voice turns off, the page stream that opened it ends (so a reload needs a new tap), or the hub stops; a restart onto a new build closes it with its own code instead (Across a hub restart, below).
- **Speech on the phone** (PRD R119). A socket's `hello` moves the voice to the phone and to the page's selected conversation. While the phone has the voice, the voice process (`packages/voice/src/phone.ts`) sends each playback, alert line and needs-you sound to the phone through the hub instead of this computer's speakers, never more than a short lead ahead of what the phone reports its output took, so Stop, a move or a cancel takes effect quickly. The lead counts from what the output took, not from what was heard after the output's latency, because a Bluetooth output's latency can be longer than the lead, which would then never let more audio go. A playback's position, interruption point, what was said and its end count from the phone's reports of what was heard, never from what was sent. A phone that reports no progress while a playback waits, for longer than the page waits for its audio to resume, fails every phone playback and loses the voice. Because iOS lets audio start only in a tap, the tap that asks for the voice creates and resumes the page's audio context.
  - **While the phone has it** only the phone page's own focus is the voice's focus, never a page on this computer, and another paired page can neither take the voice nor bring it back to this computer.
  - **Route changes**: iOS briefly interrupts or suspends the page's audio when its route changes, as Bluetooth earbuds do between playback and play-and-record. The page asks its audio to resume until it runs again, and the playback goes on from where it stopped, keeping the voice and the mic; only audio that stays stopped loses the phone. Mic on's tap sets the audio session to play and record before the audio starts, so the session's mode does not change under a playback while the mic is on; the phone's capture stays open while the voice plays.
  - **Losing the phone**: the socket closing, the page hidden for a few seconds, or its audio interrupted or suspended for a few seconds (a call, Siri) moves the voice to no device: playback stops, the mic goes off, nothing plays on this computer's speakers, and this computer's pages' voice controls show it (the phone's page says why, except for a hidden page, whose loss needs no word), until a tap on the phone, or on this computer Talk here, the mic, Voice on the computer or a message typed to the voice target's session in a page there, takes the voice back. Voice off or Mic off on the phone, even one that cuts a starting Mic on short, shows no message on the phone. The page's list header adds an untoned note only while the phone was **lost** (`VoiceControl.phoneLost`), never for voice off by the operator's choice: the page sends `leave` before it closes the socket for Voice off or a hidden page's loss and when it unloads, and `visibility {hidden}` on each change, and the hub counts a close as a loss unless the page said `leave`, was hidden, or the hub closed the socket with its refusal code (a sign-out, a forbidden device, remote access dropping it, voice turned off). The page stream ending closes the socket as going away (1001), not refused, since that may be a dropped connection. A stall the voice process reports, a resume that does not come in time and a voice process that greets a fresh hub with another device are losses; a handover carries whether the voice was lost. A new voice process is told the current device. A hub that starts anew, not by a restart's handover, keeps the voice process's device when it is not this computer's, as none, because the phone's socket closed with the hub that stopped, so it never moves the voice to this computer's speakers either. Once the hub has chosen the device itself, as by Talk here before the process's greeting comes, the process is told the hub's device instead.
  - **Across a hub restart** onto a new build ([Hub lifecycle](#hub-lifecycle)): the voice process, which outlives the hub, keeps its device, target and mic, and the hub that takes over starts from the state the old hub handed over. The old hub closes the voice socket with the restart code; the phone's page keeps its audio context and microphone, and once its stream reconnects with a new page key it opens the voice socket again with `hello {resume: true}`, with no tap. The new hub accepts that only from the paired device whose sign-in held the voice, still allowed, within 30 seconds (`PHONE_RESUME_MS`); it refuses any other resume, and a tap on any allowed device takes the voice as usual. Until the phone is back, no page on this computer is the voice's focus, a reply that asked to be heard waits and plays once the phone is back, as do the replies that came while the hubs changed over, and nothing plays on this computer; the target stays a target though no page shows it until the phone's page shows a conversation again; only an explicit action on this computer (Talk here, the mic, Voice on the computer) takes the voice there. A phone that does not come back in time leaves the voice off, as when the phone is lost.
  - **Across a dropped connection**, as a cellular blip or the page's stream lost: the hub treats a voice socket that closes as going away or abnormally, rather than refused, left by Voice off, hidden or stalled, as it treats one closed for a restart: the voice stays on the phone for `PHONE_RESUME_MS`, replies that ask to be heard wait, and nothing plays on this computer. The phone's page keeps its audio and microphone and opens the socket again with `hello {resume: true}`, every 2 s on its page key and at once on a new one, until it is welcomed, refused or the wait ends; the hub also takes a resume from the sign-in that holds the voice while its old socket has not yet closed, as on a half-open connection.
- **Dictation on the phone** (PRD R119). On a paired device's page the mic is the phone's own microphone; its tap takes the voice to the phone when it is not there and opens the microphone in the same tap. The hub refuses a paired page's Mic on unless that page holds the voice, so a paired device never turns on this computer's microphone. The page sends 16 kHz PCM only while the voice process has the phone's capture open, and the hub relays it only while that page holds the voice. The phone's busy state (no track, a muted or ended track, a hidden page) stands in for this computer's other-app watcher, so this computer's watcher and microphone are never opened for the phone. Phone capture counts as capture that cancels the echo, since the page asks for iOS's voice processing, which cancels the page's own playback: it listens while the voice plays from the first moment, so talking over a reply stops it and the words are heard. What echo is left is rejected by barge-in itself, which passes only the enrolled voice beating the reply voice's print, or without an enrolment only words not in the reply, and the words held over a reply are dropped after it as its echo; the echo check does not run on the phone, and the loudspeaker's leftover echo is not measured ([mechanics.md](mechanics.md) M127). The voice check scores phone audio against the existing voiceprint; enrolment is refused while the voice is not on this computer. Every move of the voice turns the mic off, so the mic is turned on again where the voice now is. In the phone's layout, leaving the conversation for the list turns a mic that is on for it off, since the operator has left the work item; speech already playing goes on.
- **Alerts, the needs-you sound and the voice check on the phone** (PRD R119). While the phone has the voice, the focused conversation that holds an alert back is the phone page's selected one, never a page on this computer, and a spoken alert or the needs-you sound goes out only while that page is in front; otherwise neither is sent, a waiting alert is dropped rather than kept for the page's return, and the pages keep the unread counts, and no other page plays its own needs-you sound in the voice's place. An alert's answer is heard through the phone's microphone and goes to no session, and the phone's audio gets the voice check against the voiceprint enrolled on this computer. **Voice on the computer**, on this computer's page only while the voice is on a phone or none has it, takes the voice back as Talk here does, with the mic off, and is refused with why when voice is off or the voice is already here.
- **Installing.** The manifest's `start_url`, `scope` and `id` are `/page/`, relative to the origin, and the files needed to install the page need no cookie, so a phone installs the page from the remote host over HTTPS, which a service worker needs; the phone draws the page's phone layout ([Web page](#web-page)).
- **Security.** Tailscale's WireGuard tunnel and certificate give the HTTPS. The hub trusts `tailscale serve`'s headers only because the proxy runs on this machine and connects from the loopback, and it never takes a proxied request for this computer's own. A process on this computer can send any header, as it can already reach `127.0.0.1`, but still needs a token or a sign-in. Another person on the tailnet, a device shared into it, a tagged device or a Funnel visitor carries another login or none and is refused before any route, a pairing link included; a device of the operator's own login still needs pairing. A paired device acts as the operator, except for the local-only commands, until its sign-in goes unused for 30 days or is revoked, so a lost phone is signed out by revoking its sign-in.

## Session reports

Reports from a definitively failed start with no native identity are refused; Retry must create a new session before it reports.

### Handled assignments (R85)

`report handled <sessionId> <ticket URL> [--release <assignment key>]` posts to `/handled`. It registers another ticket's current assignment as already handled by the reporting session, so Switchboard creates, triages and starts no work of its own for that ticket while the registration lasts. The report contract offers it to every session, for use only when a skill directs it. The response contains `assignmentKey`, which the skill keeps for an optional conditional release. Registration requires an open target assigned to the operator and the current session of an open work item, and cannot target the reporting work item's own ticket.

`tickets.handledAssignmentKey` is the only added storage. Events and changes keep the registration and clearing evidence, with the reporting session and work item, and deleting the reporting work item preserves it. After every accepted event, core clears each key whose reporting work item is done, deleted or missing, notes the ended keys for History, and creates a work item for a still-assigned ticket, so no ticket stays hidden after the work that handled it ends. Core refuses ticket identity adoption of an active handled target; consolidation of an inactive target transfers its audit. Ended-key audit prevents a delayed registration retry from restoring handling.

`Source.assignmentAt(url)` returns the ticket, its opaque operator assignment key and fresh queue eligibility, or a read error. For GitHub the key identifies the issue and the event that began the current continuous assignment across the configured operator logins, read from assignees and assignment events and never inferred from status history; the project's current status corrects stale search eligibility. Incomplete history, changed pagination snapshots and failed reads are errors.

Registration and polling are serialized per source. Every poll reads registered targets directly, including those missing from project search, and applies assignment reconciliation with the poll atomically. Core guards work creation, triage and session dispatch. Registration refuses a target whose open work already has a session, including an emitted start; otherwise it removes unstarted duplicates before acknowledging. A new assignment clears the key and permits normal work. An explicit release compares the supplied key and uses freshly read eligibility before scheduling work.

### Report transport

Sessions report through the hub bundle's CLI, which posts to the hub's loopback HTTP server. The prompt carries the Switchboard session ID and the bundle's absolute path, because a background session has no Switchboard environment and `switchboard` is not on PATH. `node` below is `sessions.nodePath`; a session keeps the command its prompt holds. Bash, PowerShell and cmd all run the command, and they agree only on a first word with no spaces, quotes or shell characters, so `sessions.nodePath` must be such a path to `node` or `node.exe`.

```
node "<home>/hub.js" report stage <sessionId> <stage>
node "<home>/hub.js" report worktree <sessionId> <path>
node "<home>/hub.js" report done <sessionId>
node "<home>/hub.js" report approval <sessionId> --prompt <prompt> [--summary <text>] [--report <path>]
node "<home>/hub.js" report link <sessionId> --label <text> --target <url>
node "<home>/hub.js" report playbook <sessionId> <name>
node "<home>/hub.js" report paste <sessionId> --label <text> --file <absolute path>
node "<home>/hub.js" report task <sessionId> --prompt <prompt> [--repo <repo root>] [--title <text>]
node "<home>/hub.js" report question <sessionId> --text <question>
node "<home>/hub.js" register --title <text>
```

- The contract asks for the approval summary; the CLI and the hub accept its omission. The hub refuses a report path that is not absolute; the CLI resolves a relative one against its working directory.
- Git Bash rewrites an argument that starts with `/` to a path under its Windows root, so `/sb-ship 16416` would arrive as `C:/Program Files/Git/sb-ship 16416`; under Git Bash the CLI restores an approval or task prompt that starts with that root (mechanics M20).
- `report ticket <sessionId> <url> [--playbook <name>]` (PRD R83) moves the reporting session to the ticket at the URL. It is outside the report contract's kinds, so no session's stored reports or contract text change. The hub refuses a retired copy, and refuses when no enabled source answers for the URL in time, the lookup fails, or the ticket is closed, because the move needs the ticket's title and state. On success the CLI prints the new key and the session's contract, which `/contract` also restores after compaction; a retry prints them again.
- `register` (PRD R81; mechanics M37) registers the Claude session that runs it, identified by `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID`, and prints its key and report contract. A native ID that is already a session's current one answers that session again, as does one that an earlier request registered while this one read the roster, so a retry after a lost reply recovers the contract; a native ID that is a retired or related copy of a session is refused; otherwise the Claude runner must find the session live in `claude agents --json`, or the registration is refused.
- The CLI's home is the directory that holds its `hub.js`, so a session started by a hub with `--home` reports to that home. The CLI finds the hub through `hub.json` and never starts one. Repeating a report is safe, because each report sets a value and a repeated `task` adds nothing.
- Exit codes: 0 recorded, 1 usage, 2 no hub, 3 refused (an unknown session, a report the session's contract does not offer, a link its ticket's source refuses, or a path that `git worktree list` in that path does not show as a worktree). A worktree can belong to a different repository than the source's repo root. With no hub, the work item ends `blocked` and the operator marks it done; the hub stays up while sessions are active, so this is rare.
- Done check (PRD R10): before applying a task session's `done`, the hub polls the ticket's source, within the CLI's timeout. When the poll still lists the ticket as assigned, the session's outcome is recorded as an unconfirmed done and the work item's status stays; the hub polls again after a delay, because GitHub issue search can list an issue as assigned for seconds after its unassignment (mechanics M34). Still assigned then blocks the work item; otherwise it is done. A failed or slow read, or a source turned off, accepts the report. A resume, a later approval or done report, or Mark done makes the pending check change nothing, and a hub that stopped schedules the pending checks again at startup. A poll that finds the ticket outside the assigned state marks such a work item done even while its session is still active. A setup session or an ad-hoc ticket skips the check. Reads of one source run one at a time, so an earlier scheduled poll cannot apply after the check's poll.
- A report for a work item that is already done does not reopen it: `done` sets the session's outcome, `worktree` links the ticket's worktree, `link` adds the ticket's link, and `stage`, `approval`, `playbook` and `paste` are ignored.
- `playbook` names a task playbook in the work item's namespace and sets the work item's playbook only, so the session keeps the reports its prompt offered.
- `task` (PRD R68) creates an ad-hoc ticket and an `unstarted` work item whose parent is the reporting session's work item, whether or not that work item is done. Its repo is `--repo` or the reporting ticket's root ([Repo roots](#repo-roots)) and must be one of the configured repo roots, compared ignoring case and separators. A report whose prompt equals that of an open work item from the same parent adds nothing.
- `link` takes only an http(s) URL. A link to a target the ticket already has replaces its label.
- `paste` (PRD R60) takes a non-empty label and a `--file` that names one file wherever it is read (on Windows a drive or UNC path; elsewhere a POSIX absolute path), because the hub reads the file later, when Copy asks for it, outside the session's working directory; the CLI and the hub refuse any other. The hub stores it on the session's work item when the session is the latest, replacing a paste with the same label.

## Friction debriefs

PRD R55, ported from Ground Control's debrief runner (GC M64). Off unless `sessions.debriefs` is true. The hub asks a finished or idle Switchboard session, through a fork of it, what friction it met, and records the answer for the friction-review prompt to read.

- **Files:** under the Claude root (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or `~/.claude`): the prompt `skills/friction-review/prompt.md`, the state `.wip/debrief/state.json` and the log `.wip/debrief/log/YYYY-MM.jsonl`. They are the files Ground Control wrote, in the same format, so Ground Control's record still holds and a reader of the log finds both runners' lines. A session's entry has one writer, the hub that started the session.
- **Candidates:** a started session with a native ID is due once its work item is done, or once its last turn has been idle long enough that the operator is unlikely to resume it, and is skipped once the one-hour prompt cache has expired rather than run cold. It also needs enough new tool calls since its last debriefed message, in a range whose debrief has not failed. One fork runs at a time, oldest transcript first.
- **Claude sessions:** before each fork the hub reads `claude agents --json` and skips a session that is running; an idle background session and a stopped one can be forked (mechanics M19). The fork resumes the session as a fork that is not persisted, in its `cwd`, with the prompt on stdin and `FRICTION_DEBRIEF=1`, and with no model, effort, tool or MCP flag, because each changes the prompt prefix and loses the cache. The prompt names the range to cover and the resumable subagents with new work, whose transcripts are copied where the fork looks for them for the run.
- **Codex sessions:** a Codex session's own thread is a candidate on the same terms, read from its rollout, and is forked with `codex exec fork`, read-only and ephemeral. Codex threads that a Claude session delegated to through agent-delegate are marked pending by that session's debrief and asked once each through agent-delegate's debrief command; a missing command skips them.
- **Answer:** the fork's last result, `{"friction": [...]}` or a bare array, each item with non-empty `what`, `source`, `workaround`, `cost` and `evidence`.
- **Record:** a success appends a log line and then merges into the state as it is on disk, so another writer's entries are kept. A failure records the range and its error in the state and the hub log and is not retried; the next debrief covers that range with any later work. A write that fails is held, and no fork starts until it is written. A hub that stops between the two writes asks about that range again.

## Settings

`settings.json` in the config folder, plain JSON, holds the settings and the [playbooks](#playbooks). The operator owns it; this repository holds none of the operator's values. The hub validates it on load and on change (PRD R42): any error outside `playbooks` rejects the whole file and keeps the last valid settings and playbooks, while each playbook is checked on its own. `parseSettings` in `packages/core/src/settings.ts` and each source package's config parser are the authority for keys, values and defaults; the web page's Settings edits them (Editing, below).

Settings owned by other sections: `alerts` (PRD R65; [Needs-you alerts](#needs-you-alerts)), `sessions.codeMode` (PRD R93; [Code Mode sessions](#code-mode-sessions)), `voice` (PRD R105; [Voice](#voice)), `web.port` (PRD R109; [Web page](#web-page)), `web.iframeBrowser` (PRD R125; [Embedded link tabs](#embedded-link-tabs)), `web.diffLayout` and `web.diffWrap` (PRD R118), `web.messageTimes` ([Conversation view](#conversation-view), Timeline), `web.remoteHosts`, `web.remoteLogins` and `web.tailscale` (PRD R115; [Remote access](#remote-access)), and `schedules` (PRD R82), whose playbook must be a task playbook that needs no worktree, because a scheduled run has no source `setupPrompt`.

- `sources` (PRD R3): each key names a source, in lowercase letters, digits and hyphens, and not `schedule`; its `kind`, defaulting to the name, picks the source plugin. Polls, tickets, toggles and errors are keyed by the name. A kind with no plugin disables that source. The file is rejected whole for a source named after another kind, and two GitHub sources that read the same project board, including one that is off, because an issue would then belong to both.
- A source implements core's `Source` (`packages/core/src/source.ts`) and only reads: `poll(current)` returns a complete read or an error, given the source's tickets the store lists as assigned or in the pool, and a failed read changes nothing; `context(ticket)` returns the triage context; `keyOfBranch(branch, repo)` reads a ticket key from a worktree branch. The optional `ticketAt(url)` reads the ticket a ticket report names, or answers null for a URL that is not one of its tickets (PRD R83); `assignmentAt(url, anyBoard)` reads the operator's actual assignment (PRD R85), and the poll's reread of a registered ticket passes `anyBoard`, so a registered issue that left the board can still be released. The hub asks the source that stores a ticket with the URL first, and refuses while that source is configured but off. A source with a pool marks each polled ticket `assigned`, `pool` or neither.
- Pick (PRD R48): a picked support case starts its playbook's task session; Switchboard does not claim the case.
- GitHub keys (PRD R44): a ticket key is the bare issue number in the source's home repository and `owner/name#n` elsewhere, and every read of a stored ticket goes to its key's repository. A pull request link is accepted when a closing reference names the ticket, or, in the ticket's repository, when its branch names the ticket's key. Because bare keys mean the home repository, the hub records each GitHub source's home once it has tickets and refuses a settings change to it. A repository label may not equal a source name, `gh`, `adhoc` or `schedule`, nor label two repositories.
- One source per issue (PRD R44): a polled ticket whose URL already has a ticket in another source is dropped and noted in the snapshot, which the page's list header shows with the errors.
- Start gate (PRD R44): a triaged work item whose ticket's repository is not its source's home repository waits for Start, whatever the playbook's `autoStart`.
- Display keys (PRD R27): rows show a ticket outside the home repository with its repository's label or name as a prefix, while the stored key still names windows, workspace files, terminals, sessions and History.
- Worktree linking (PRD R25): after each poll the hub lists worktrees in the root of the home repository and of each repository with an assigned, pool or registered ticket in the source, and reads each branch with that repository.
- GitHub reads go through `gh api graphql`. The issue search is scoped by the project board (mechanics M59), then filtered on project status; an issue on another status is not assigned (mechanics M2). With `includeClosed` the search also returns closed issues, which then count as assigned and open (mechanics M60). With `requireAssignee` false (PRD R44) the search has no assignee qualifier and lists only issues in scope, so a poll creates no ticket for an issue never in scope; their scope entry is null (PRD R53). Otherwise the scope entry (PRD R53) is the start of the operator's current continuous assignment, from the assignment events, and the sort time is the later of that and the Status field's `updatedAt`, because status-change events have been missing from the issue timeline since about 2026-09-25. A poll that needs more pages than `maxPages` fails. Each stored assigned ticket that the search leaves out or lists outside scope is read on its own, because issue search can return a successful but incomplete result during a GitHub outage (mechanics M48); a failed read fails the poll. The triage context holds the board status, the issue's body, recent comments and assignment and status history, and a linked pull request's state, reviews, checks and recent comments and threads, each text clipped keeping its start and end. On somebody else's pull request, one line counts the operator's own submitted reviews and pull request comments, so triage can choose between a first review and a follow-up without reading the lists (mechanics M58). Bots show as `<login> (bot)`, since a bot's GraphQL login has no `[bot]` suffix. Comment headings carry an anchor ID, and the optional `rolesFile` adds each listed author's roles; a roles file that cannot be read leaves the roles out, the hub logs a warning, and triage continues. The context read also returns the linked pull request as a link, which a successful triage adds to the ticket's links, keeping earlier ones (PRD R52).
- With `waitForBlockers` (PRD R90) the source keeps each issue's open blockers, keeping only `OPEN` ones because GitHub also lists closed blockers (mechanics M56); with it off every listed issue gets no blockers, which clears stored ones. An issue with more blockers than one read holds loses the rest.
- GitHub errors: offline, timeout and rate limits are temporary; the poll changes nothing and runs again at the next interval. Missing `gh` or failed authentication shows in the page's list header. Each poll first checks that the configured status field exists and is a single-select field, and fails otherwise, because the poll would read every issue's status as null and end their work items (mechanics M17).
- Zoho reads use the Desk REST API with the access token `tokenCommand` prints and the configured organization ID. IDs stay strings. Open tickets are filtered by `scopeField` and `scopeValue`; the operator’s agent is assigned, configured `poolTeams` and `automationAgentIds` count as unclaimed, and other holders are left out.
