A failed confirming ticket read or team read fails the poll without changing stored state. Scope entry uses `scopeHistoryField` when set and status changes; context and history fields come from settings. The source’s config parser owns supported keys and defaults.
- Existing Zoho settings must rename `escalationField` and `escalationValue` to `scopeField` and `scopeValue`, set `tokenCommand`, and list the teams whose members leave tickets in the pool in `poolTeams`; list every Desk team to preserve the former all-team-members rule. Set the former automation IDs and context fields explicitly to retain them.
- `plugins` lists absolute or home-relative module paths. The hub imports each loaded module once per process, validates its export and rejects duplicate kinds. Missing modules are retried on a settings check, save or reload; a module that failed to evaluate needs a hub restart. A rejected settings file keeps its plugins and settings in force together. A reload overtaken by a newer reload applies nothing. Settings check and save resolve draft plugins before reading source state; they never activate draft plugins.
- Plugin modules own their source-specific reads and configuration. Built-in and external sources share the same read-only interface; the hub sends labels and field descriptions to the Settings editor. Plugins may supply a filled SVG path with a view box, optional hex color, width and fill rule.
- Commands run through `bash -c`. On Windows the hub finds Git Bash from `CLAUDE_CODE_GIT_BASH_PATH` or beside `git.exe` on PATH.
- Tickets from different sources about the same case remain separate (PRD R26). A source’s triage context may return links it finds in ticket text (PRD R61), distinct from links a session reports as its own work.


Editing (PRD R54): the web page's Settings (`packages/web/src/settings.tsx`, with its state, fields and pages in `settingsModel.ts`, ported from the retired dock's Settings window) edits settings.json through the hub, on this computer's page only ([Web page](#web-page), Settings).

- **Hub owns the file.** The hub reads, checks and writes settings.json; the page commands are `settingsRoutes` in the hub. `page-settings-read` answers the file (its path, version, value and any error), the config folder and what the editor offers: permission modes, providers, template variables, source kinds, the open work items per playbook and which sources are on. `page-settings-check {settings}` answers `{errors}`. `page-settings-save {value, version, configDir}` writes the whole file as formatted JSON and answers `{saved, version}`, or 409 `{errors}` when the file changed since `version`, the folder is not the hub's current config folder, or the settings have errors. `page-voice-enroll` starts, reads and cancels the voice enrolment ([Voice check and barge-in](#voice-check-and-barge-in)).
- **One authority for errors.** Checks (`checkSettings` in `packages/hub/src/config.ts`) apply what the hub applies on load: the settings parser, each enabled source's own parser and each playbook's, plus a check of template variables, because the hub otherwise finds an unknown variable only when rendering fails. A source's errors count only while it is on, so a source that is off can be saved incomplete. A playbook's errors read `playbooks.<name>: <error>`.
- **Retired settings.** `dock` and `web.chromePane`, which the retired dock, VS Code extension and Chrome pane read, and `alerts.surface: dock`, load with a warning and no effect for one release (`settingsWarnings`), so an old file still loads.
- **Optimistic concurrency.** A file's version is the SHA-256 of its text. A save names the version its draft started from and is refused when the file on disk no longer matches; a successful save answers the version it wrote, so editing continues over it. Writes go through a temporary file and a rename, so the hub's reload never reads a half-written file. The hub's watcher reloads the written file as it does a hand edit, and then sends `config` to every page, which reads the file again.
- **Draft.** Settings holds one draft of the whole file and never saves on its own. The draft is checked 250 ms after the last edit and after each read, and each error shows on its field, one that names no field at the top of the page with settings.json's path, where it must be fixed. Save is refused while the file changed on disk under the draft or while any error stands, and waits for a due check; a refusal keeps the draft. A draft equal to the file is dropped on a read; any other draft whose file changed is kept with the changed-on-disk warning and cannot be saved, so the operator discards it to take the file on disk; another config folder drops the draft and says so. The page reads the file again on `config` and when it gets the focus or becomes visible, for an event missed while its stream was down. Save closes Settings once the file holds the draft, Ctrl+S saves and stays open, Discard drops the draft and closes, and closing with unsaved changes, from Close or Escape, asks Save, Discard or Keep editing.
- **What a save writes.** A field left empty removes its key, so the default applies, except where the default is not empty, such as `historyFields`, whose emptied list is written as `[]`. Keys Settings has no field for are kept. An entry Settings cannot read into fields shows its error, so the operator fixes it by hand or deletes it. Removing a source deletes its key and leaves its work items.
- **Pages.** The navigation groups the pages under labelled categories: Work (Sessions, Triage, Sources, Playbooks by namespace, Schedules), App (Alerts and display, Devices, Advanced) and Voice (Spoken replies, Dictation, Personas, named after PRD terms, operator decision 2026-11-25). A list, such as Sources or a namespace's playbooks, nests under its entry with its New entries, closed unless it holds the page shown, and counts its pages while closed; a namespace's entry opens its table too. A page with unsaved changes shows a dot in it, and one with errors an error mark, which a closed list carries for the pages in it and for an entry removed from it. Each page is one task, with one line under its title saying what it is for: Sessions holds the defaults for all agents and for Claude and Codex; Triage the classifier; Alerts and display where needs-you alerts show, the needs-you sound, Show message times and notes (`web.messageTimes`) beside Fold reply details (`voice.briefReplies`) under Conversations, and how diffs show, with a line leading to the spoken alerts; Devices the paired devices and remote access; Advanced the experiments, the programs Switchboard runs, a developer's debriefs and where settings.json and the config folder are, the only place the file's path shows; Spoken replies, marked Experimental beside its title with a line saying they may be unreliable, that replies are spoken only while Speak replies is on and that spoken alerts have their own switch, the voice's switch, Use voice (`voice.enabled`, which starts the voice process for spoken replies and dictation alike), the speaker, Speak replies (no settings.json key: the switch shows and sets the hub's Spoken replies at once, with no save, and is disabled while the hub has no voice), how replies are spoken, and Spoken alerts (`voice.spokenAlerts`); Dictation the microphone with the voice enrolment, dictation, When you speak over a spoken reply (`voice.duplex`), Another person speaks near the mic (the room hold), and under Advanced `voice.matchThreshold`; Personas a persona select for each task playbook by namespace, and the assignments whose playbook is missing, None removing an assignment. While the voice is off, Dictation, Personas and the spoken alerts say so above their dimmed settings, with a link to the switch. The voice check's `voice.pitchLowFactor` and `voice.pitchMinLowShare` have no field: they are tuned in settings.json, and their errors show on Dictation (operator decision 2026-11-25). Pages are a view over the same settings.json keys, so several pages edit one object, such as `sessions` or `web`, and a page is marked unsaved by the keys it shows; an error that names a section but no field shows on the page for that section. A rarely changed part of a page is a collapsed Advanced section at its end, which names what it holds, stays open across redraws once opened, and opens when a check places a new error in it. Each source has a page with its kind's connection fields, the work fields and Advanced, which holds Kind; a source that is off is noted in the navigation, and changing the kind keeps only the common keys; each playbook a page with About, Start, Reports, Run as, Prompt and Advanced (Kind, Return of, Focus page), its fields following its kind, reports and agent, with a table page per namespace; each schedule a page whose day and time show only for the period that uses them; each persona has a page, and a task playbook's page has a Persona select in Run as that edits the same `voice.playbookPersonas` draft as the Personas page. Delete of a playbook asks first, naming its open work items, and an edit undone restores the entry as the file has it. Their labels, help and the composer's voice text say dictation for the operator's speech and spoken replies for Switchboard's, naming the actor, the operator as "you" and the app as "Switchboard" (PRD Design rules, operator decision 2026-11-25). Each section is a bordered card under its heading, a setting's help shows under its label in a sentence or two, with any setup walkthrough left to this document, and an on/off setting is a switch at its row's end. An empty field says what applies: "Default: <value>" for a default, "From Sessions: <value>" for a value a playbook takes from elsewhere, and "e.g. <value>" for an example, with Required beside a field the hub needs; a number's unit follows it. A persona shows by its name, its ID in a tooltip and under its page's title, and its page lists the playbooks that take it. The bar under the pages shows only when it has something to say: unsaved changes, errors, a save just made, a file that cannot be read or changed on disk, or retired settings; it offers Save and Discard only while there is something to save or discard. On a phone Settings is the whole screen and its navigation a card of entries per category that drills into each list and page, Back going up one level at a time.
- **Paired devices.** The Devices page's paired devices ([Remote access](#remote-access)) list each sign-in's name, where from, last use and whether voice is allowed, with Allow voice or Forbid voice on a paired device and Sign out, and Pair a device… shows a new link, drawn as a QR code too, and code, through `page-pair`, `page-devices` and `page-device-revoke`, which change nothing in settings.json.

The operator's playbooks live in their settings.json, not in this repository. Each runs one skill, such as `dev:develop`. Scheduled playbooks are in the `ops` namespace, which no source triages into.

## Queue volume (R84)

Removed by the operator's decision. Zoho and other pool sources return only tickets assigned to the operator or in the pool, and triage runs only for work items and pool tickets. Tickets another engineer held stay, neither assigned nor in the pool, as a ticket that left the pool does.

## Embedded link tabs

With `web.iframeBrowser` enabled (R125), the page owns the selected work item's link tabs and the extension host owns their website documents. The ordinary page shows those tabs and launches the host for the selected work item; it creates no website frames. A host is reused per hub origin and browser profile. Its trusted Switchboard child is the existing page; each visited HTTP or HTTPS link is a sibling iframe. Chromium's extension cookie exemption permits the profile's sign-in cookies for the document and its later requests without copying or rewriting cookies; site-specific sign-in flows still need verification (mechanics M138). Unsupported browsers use the external tab fallback.

The page decides by what the browser can do (`canInstallExtension` in `iframeBrowser.ts`). Where the browser can install the extension, desktop Chromium as Chrome and Edge, read from its client hints' Chromium brand and `mobile` and `platform` where it has them, else from its user agent string, the page shows the link tabs; without the extension a link tab still selects, and its pane asks for the extension, offering the link in a browser tab, the extension's download and Retry. The conversation's and History's web links take the same path: a link tab while the extension answers, else the Switchboard tab group (PRD R123) or a new tab. Where it cannot, as Safari, Firefox, every iOS and iPadOS browser and Chromium on Android, the page makes no Browser or link tabs and never asks for the extension, unless the extension answers all the same; the work item's and the conversation's links open as ordinary links, in a new tab.

The web page's Download app shortcut action works locally and on paired devices. Its authenticated download gives a zip containing one Windows `.lnk` or one Linux desktop entry, bound to the request's validated hub origin; the browser and profile/installation choices cannot replace that origin or add arbitrary command-line arguments. The `.lnk` names Chrome or Edge through Windows environment variables resolved on the receiving computer; the desktop entry names `google-chrome` on the receiving computer's PATH, and its `StartupWMClass` is the app ID Chrome gives the window on Wayland (mechanics M160). Each launches that extension document with `--app`. It contains no pairing token, hub-computer path, installer or script. The shortcut selects an explicit profile, Default unless changed, because its Windows app identity must match the browser window; no extension API reveals that directory automatically. Before downloading, the extension verifies the requesting page's configured origin and live embedding access, saves the packaged Switchboard icon, an ICO for Windows or a PNG for Linux, through the browser's Downloads API and returns its actual client-side path. The shortcut references that file and can move independently of it. The extension host supplies its own multi-size favicon, since its embedded child's icon cannot brand the outer document.

The host remembers only exact hub origins after successful live origin, setting and sign-in checks. On a transport outage, a remembered origin may load the cached Switchboard shell, but the host ignores website frame commands until live verification succeeds. Unknown origins, explicit refusal and malformed answers fail closed; refusal also forgets the saved origin. Verification is serialized, bounded in time and repeated while open and after reconnection. The inner page retains its service worker and reconnect behavior; no API data is cached. This is an app-window launch, not PWA registration.

The page sends the whole desired list with a work item, increasing sequence, selected original URL and bounded visible rectangle. The host validates the sender window and origin, safe item and sequence values, distinct bounded HTTP or HTTPS URLs without embedded credentials and the selected URL's membership before reconciling. The page waits for the site rules before showing a new host. Links to the trusted hub, its loopback alias or fixed browser endpoint open outside instead. Until a website paints, its frame shows the web page theme's editor background and colour scheme, dark or light by `prefers-color-scheme` as the page's theme follows it (`iframeHost.html`), rather than white; a site that paints its own white background is white once it paints. It creates only the selected document, keeps inactive frames attached, invisible and inert with their viewport size preserved, and never resets an existing frame's source when switching tabs or showing overlays. Tab identity uses the work item and original URL, independent of later in-frame navigation. Closing a tab or changing work items removes obsolete frames; temporary connection or extension failures and missed heartbeats hide them. The website frame lies over the Switchboard child, so the page's layers would draw under it. Instead of hiding the frame, the host cuts out of it the box of each menu, popover, dialog, toast, the Update ready card and the list's drawer that overlaps its slot, so the layer shows through and takes the pointer, because a clipped area takes no pointer events (mechanics M152). Under a modal backdrop the host darkens the frame by the backdrop's alpha, makes it inert and passes the pointer to the page, whose backdrop takes the press; an opaque backdrop or more layers than the host accepts hide the frame. The page sends the boxes (with each one's corner radius), darkening and modal flag with each placement (`frameCover` in `iframeBrowser.ts`), again when its layers change or resize, and each frame while one slides or fades; the host validates and bounds them and builds one even-odd `clip-path` (`holesClip` in `iframeFrames.ts`), dropping a box inside another's rounded shape and splitting overlapping ones into square-cornered rectangles so that a cut never fills again. The page and the extension update separately, so the host answers each accepted placement with `switchboard-iframe-holes`; until it has, the page keeps the older behavior for an extension that cuts nothing: it hides the frame under a modal dialog, the drawer and Settings, and under a menu, popover, toast or the Update ready card while its box overlaps the slot, showing it again `FRAME_SHOW_DELAY_MS` after the last such layer closes.

The extension accepts requests only from its configured Switchboard page in a verified tab. The local fixed browser endpoint must vouch for its actual page origins; another loopback server cannot vouch for itself. Remote copies accept only their configured HTTPS origin and check the page's sign-in. The Chrome extension requests host access to HTTP and HTTPS sites. Session header rules match only that tab, its top-level extension domain and the exact request hosts and ports in the current tab list, with the extension, that host or its subdomains as the navigation initiator. Nested cross-site frames retain their framing restrictions. A higher-priority allow rule protects the trusted hub origin and its loopback alias, including the port, so Switchboard retains its CSP while other local sites can be framed. They remove X-Frame-Options and the entire CSP, including protections beyond frame-ancestors. Zoho Desk answers its own navigations from a service worker, which fetches them outside the tab, so for the hosts the extension lists (`IFRAME_WORKER_HOSTS`, only `desk.zoho.com`; operator decision 2026-11-24) a second rule removes only X-Frame-Options from that host's own service-worker fetches (no tab, the xmlhttprequest type, the extension or the site as top domain and initiator); this rule leaves CSP alone, so ordinary tabs keep theirs, and a CSP frame-ancestors refusal served from a service worker still refuses. A worker fetch has no tab and takes its top domain from its initiator, so while the lease lasts this also lets any other site frame that host's worker-served pages (mechanics M153). Ordinary browser tabs and cookies are unchanged, apart from the service-worker exception above. Leases survive service-worker restarts, expire without renewal, and are removed on tab close, navigation, configuration or sign-in failure. Chrome/Edge 145 or later is required for top-level-domain rules.

Website frames have no extension API bridge and cannot navigate the host's top-level page. Only the trusted app child may reconcile or place them. The host delegates microphone, clipboard, autoplay and fullscreen to that child, and clipboard writing and fullscreen to GitHub. It mirrors the app title, reports focus for the whole host so working in a website frame keeps the voice on the page, and opens as a normal tab. Cross-origin desktop notification prompts require the ordinary page. Turning the setting off returns to that page; changing its framing policy requires a reload. HTML cache validators include that policy so a reload cannot retain an earlier framing refusal or permission. Authentication results and browser limitations remain dated evidence in mechanics.md.
